- **Score**: 6/10

### Resolving Turbulent Magnetohydrodynamics: A Hybrid Operator-Diffusion Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02106v1)
- **Authors**: Semih Kacmaz, E. A. Huerta, Roland Haas
- **Abstract**: We present a hybrid machine learning framework that combines Physics-Informed Neural Operators (PINOs) with score-based generative diffusion models to simulate the full spatio-temporal evolution of two-dimensional, incompressible, resistive magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence across a broad range of Reynolds numbers ($\mathrm{Re}$). The framework leverages the equation-constrained generalization capabilities of PINOs to predict coherent, low-frequency dynamics, while a conditional diffusion model stochastically corrects high-frequency residuals, enabling accurate modeling of fully developed turbulence. Trained on a comprehensive ensemble of high-fidelity simulations with $\mathrm{Re} \in \{100, 250, 500, 750, 1000, 3000, 10000\}$, the approach achieves state-of-the-art accuracy in regimes previously inaccessible to deterministic surrogates. At $\mathrm{Re}=1000$ and $3000$, the model faithfully reconstructs the full spectral energy distributions of both velocity and magnetic fields late into the simulation, capturing non-Gaussian statistics, intermittent structures, and cross-field correlations with high fidelity. At extreme turbulence levels ($\mathrm{Re}=10000$), it remains the first surrogate capable of recovering the high-wavenumber evolution of the magnetic field, preserving large-scale morphology and enabling statistically meaningful predictions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided paper and will offer a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and a justified novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a hybrid machine learning framework that combines Physics-Informed Neural Operators (PINOs) with score-based generative diffusion models to simulate two-dimensional, incompressible, resistive magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence across a range of Reynolds numbers.  The PINO component predicts coherent, low-frequency dynamics using equation-constrained learning, while the conditional diffusion model stochastically corrects high-frequency residuals, enabling more accurate modeling of fully developed turbulence. The authors train the model on high-fidelity MHD simulations across a range of Reynolds numbers and demonstrate its effectiveness in reconstructing energy spectra, capturing non-Gaussian statistics, and recovering intermittent structures at high Reynolds numbers, surpassing the capabilities of previous deterministic surrogate models. The proposed approach is called Diffusion-Integrated Neural Operators (DINOs).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The primary novelty lies in the synergistic combination of PINOs and diffusion models for MHD turbulence simulation.  Previous works had explored PINOs for MHD, but they were limited by their ability to capture high-frequency turbulent details at high Reynolds numbers. Diffusion models have shown promise in generating turbulent fields from limited information, but their application to *temporal dynamics* and MHD's coupled fields is less explored. Using a PINO to provide a structured, physically-informed prior *before* using a diffusion model to add the fine-grained detail represents a significant departure from existing approaches.
    *   **Weaknesses:** Hybrid approaches (PINOs + diffusion models) *have* been explored in other contexts (as the paper itself acknowledges), diminishing the novelty slightly. The specific architecture is an extension of existing ones in each field. The core idea is to leverage each model for its strength rather than designing a fundamentally new model, which is generally a good engineering approach but potentially impacts the magnitude of the novelty.
    *   The application to the specific physical system (MHD) adds to the novelty, but does not fundamentally create it.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The ability to accurately simulate MHD turbulence at high Reynolds numbers using machine learning is highly significant.  High-fidelity simulations of MHD are computationally expensive, limiting exploration of parameter space and hindering scientific progress in areas like fusion energy and astrophysical plasmas. A fast, accurate surrogate model can accelerate research in these areas by orders of magnitude. The paper's demonstration that the hybrid model captures important statistical properties of turbulence, such as energy spectra and intermittency, further enhances its significance.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The paper focuses on a *simplified* 2D incompressible resistive MHD regime. While challenging, extending the framework to more complex 3D, compressible, or non-resistive MHD scenarios will be necessary to translate this work into broader applicability. It remains to be shown how well the framework generalizes to different MHD system parameters. The code is not provided immediately, which makes it difficult to verify the results independently and assess the practical applicability of the work.

*   **Justification for the Score:**

    The paper demonstrates a significant advancement in the simulation of MHD turbulence by intelligently combining two existing machine learning paradigms. The results show tangible improvements over previous PINO-based approaches, particularly at higher Reynolds numbers. The framework has the potential to significantly accelerate research in various areas of plasma physics. However, the incremental nature of the architectural design, reliance on well-known building blocks, and the focus on a simplified MHD regime constrain the overall impact. The work still represents a valuable contribution, and has opened pathways for applying such techniques in complicated fluid dynamical systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** This score reflects the paper's significant improvement in MHD turbulence modeling through a well-engineered hybrid approach, balanced by the incremental nature of the architectural development and the limitations of the 2D resistive incompressible regime. The strong empirical evidence justifies a higher score, while the limitations temper it.

- **Classification**: physics.flu-dyn
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scaling Collapse Reveals Universal Dynamics in Compute-Optimally Trained Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02119v1)
- **Authors**: Shikai Qiu, Lechao Xiao, Andrew Gordon Wilson, Jeffrey Pennington, Atish Agarwala
- **Abstract**: What scaling limits govern neural network training dynamics when model size and training time grow in tandem? We show that despite the complex interactions between architecture, training algorithms, and data, compute-optimally trained models exhibit a remarkably precise universality. Specifically, loss curves from models of varying sizes collapse onto a single universal curve when training compute and loss are normalized to unity at the end of training. With learning rate decay, the collapse becomes so tight that differences in the normalized curves across models fall below the noise floor of individual loss curves across random seeds, a phenomenon we term supercollapse. We observe supercollapse across learning rate schedules, datasets, and architectures, including transformers trained on next-token prediction, and find it breaks down when hyperparameters are scaled suboptimally, providing a precise and practical indicator of good scaling. We explain these phenomena by connecting collapse to the power-law structure in typical neural scaling laws, and analyzing a simple yet surprisingly effective model of SGD noise dynamics that accurately predicts loss curves across various learning rate schedules and quantitatively explains the origin of supercollapse.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Scaling Collapse Reveals Universal Dynamics in Compute-Optimally Trained Neural Networks":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "scaling collapse" and "supercollapse" in neural network training. It demonstrates empirically that when neural networks are trained in a compute-optimal regime (i.e., training time scales with model size to achieve optimal performance), their loss curves, after normalization, collapse onto a single universal curve, independent of model size, architecture, dataset, and learning rate schedule.  Supercollapse refers to an even tighter collapse, where cross-model differences in the normalized loss fall below the level of noise in the individual loss curves across random seeds.  The authors connect this phenomenon to the power-law structure of neural scaling laws and provide a simple theoretical model, based on SGD noise dynamics, that accurately predicts loss curves and explains the origin of supercollapse. The breakdown of collapse indicates poorly scaled hyperparameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The observation of a universal loss curve scaling collapse and supercollapse in compute-optimally trained neural networks is, to the best of my knowledge, novel. While previous work has explored scaling laws of *final* losses and optimal hyperparameters, and rough power-law approximations of loss curves, this paper's finding of a precisely defined scaling symmetry and a single universal curve during training (especially supercollapse) constitutes a significant incremental contribution. This precise universality is not trivially expected given the complex interactions present in modern neural network training. The concept of supercollapse provides a powerful tool for analyzing the effectiveness of scaling efforts.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper is multi-faceted. First, it offers a deeper understanding of the dynamics governing neural network training at scale. Second, it provides a practical diagnostic tool (scaling collapse/supercollapse) to identify whether hyperparameters and training setups are properly scaled. This can significantly reduce the cost of large-scale experiments by flagging issues earlier. The ability to predict loss curves and explain the variance reduction effect in supercollapse with a simple model is valuable. Finally, the paper suggests a refined approach for hyperparameter optimization by maximizing collapse quality rather than relying solely on final loss values. This method could prove to be more efficient.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Rigor:** The paper presents a strong empirical foundation, demonstrating scaling collapse across a variety of tasks, architectures (Transformers, MLPs), and learning rate schedules.
    *   **Theoretical Explanation:** The theoretical analysis, while elementary, provides a plausible explanation for the observed phenomena, connecting scaling collapse to the power-law structure of neural scaling laws and the SGD noise dynamics. The simple noise model is surprisingly effective.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights the practical value of scaling collapse/supercollapse as a diagnostic tool for identifying poorly scaled hyperparameters.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and the concepts are clearly presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplicity of the Theoretical Model:** While the simple theoretical model is effective, it relies on several approximations. A more rigorous and comprehensive theory might be needed to fully capture the complexity of neural network training. It's not clear how far this simple theory can be pushed.
    *   **Limited Scale:** While the results are compelling, the experiments are conducted on relatively small models and datasets. Further validation on larger, more practically relevant scales is warranted.
    *   **Generality of Claims:** The claims of universality need to be tempered by the fact that they are based on specific choices of architectures, optimizers, and datasets. The extent to which scaling collapse/supercollapse generalizes to other settings (e.g., different optimization algorithms, different architectural families) remains to be investigated.
    *   **The relationship between this work and sharpness needs further exploration:** The finding that the scale-invariance of the training dynamics revealed by the collapse can diagnose proper hyperparameter configuration may be related to sharpness measures and the link between sharpness and generalization.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways:
    *   **Guiding Hyperparameter Optimization:** It may lead to the development of new hyperparameter optimization strategies that focus on maximizing collapse quality.
    *   **Improving Training Efficiency:** It could contribute to the design of more efficient training pipelines by identifying and correcting scaling issues early on.
    *   **Advancing Theoretical Understanding:** It could stimulate further theoretical research into the dynamics of neural network training at scale.
    *   **Provide a benchmark for new scaling techniques:** New methods could be compared against these results for improvement.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution to our understanding of neural network training at scale. It presents a novel empirical finding (scaling collapse/supercollapse), provides a plausible theoretical explanation, and highlights the practical implications. While the theoretical model is relatively simple and the empirical validation is limited to relatively small scales, the paper's insights are significant and have the potential to influence future research and practice. The limitations in the theoretical model and scale of experiments prevent a higher rating.

### PAL: Designing Conversational Agents as Scalable, Cooperative Patient Simulators for Palliative-Care Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02122v1)
- **Authors**: Neil K. R. Sehgal, Hita Kambhamettu, Allen Chang, Andrew Zhu, Lyle Ungar, Sharath Chandra Guntuku
- **Abstract**: Effective communication in serious illness and palliative care is essential but often under-taught due to limited access to training resources like standardized patients. We present PAL (Palliative Assisted Learning-bot), a conversational system that simulates emotionally nuanced patient interactions and delivers structured feedback grounded in an existing empathy-based framework. PAL supports text and voice modalities and is designed to scaffold clinical skill-building through repeated, low-cost practice. Through a mixed-methods study with 17 U.S. medical trainees and clinicians, we explore user engagement with PAL, evaluate usability, and examine design tensions around modalities, emotional realism, and feedback delivery. Participants found PAL helpful for reflection and skill refinement, though some noted limitations in emotional authenticity and the adaptability of feedback. We contribute: (1) empirical evidence that large language models can support palliative communication training; (2) design insights for modality-aware, emotionally sensitive simulation tools; and (3) implications for systems that support emotional labor, cooperative learning, and AI-augmented training in high-stakes care settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PAL (Palliative Assisted Learning-bot), a conversational agent designed to simulate emotionally nuanced patient interactions for palliative care communication training.  PAL supports both text and voice modalities and provides structured, scenario-specific feedback grounded in the NURSE empathy framework (Naming, Understanding, Respecting, Supporting, Exploring). The authors present a mixed-methods study with 17 U.S. clinicians and trainees evaluating PAL's usability, modality design, and feedback mechanisms.  The study found that participants found PAL helpful for reflection and skill refinement, while also highlighting design tensions around emotional authenticity and feedback adaptability. The paper contributes empirical evidence supporting LLM-driven simulation in palliative care, a design framework for chatbot-mediated training, and implications for systems supporting emotional labor and clinical collaboration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*  While the application of conversational agents to medical training is not entirely novel, the paper presents a specific application to palliative care, a domain with significant emotional complexity that is often under-addressed in current simulation tools. The integration of the NURSE framework to provide structured feedback based on empathy principles is a key differentiator. The dual-modality (text and voice) approach also represents a valuable design consideration, allowing for flexibility in learning styles.  The use of LLMs specifically for *emotional* feedback and nuance is also a valuable contribution.

*Significance:* The paper addresses a critical gap in medical education – the lack of accessible and effective training in palliative care communication. The scalability and low cost of AI-driven simulations offer a potential solution to the limitations of traditional methods like standardized patients. The study's findings provide valuable design insights for creating more effective and emotionally intelligent simulation tools for healthcare professionals. The emphasis on supporting emotional labor and clinical collaboration aligns with the broader goals of improving patient-centered care. However, the sample size is relatively small, and the transfer of skills to real-world clinical practice remains to be seen. The lack of cultural diversity in the sample is also a weakness.

*Strengths:*
*   Well-defined problem and clear objectives.
*   Rigorous mixed-methods approach.
*   Specific design insights for modality and feedback mechanisms.
*   Focus on a critical and often neglected area of medical training.
*   Clear articulation of limitations and future directions.

*Weaknesses:*
*   Relatively small sample size.
*   Limited diversity in the participant pool (all US-based).
*   Lack of assessment of long-term skill retention or transfer to real clinical practice.
*   Potential limitations of the NURSE framework's applicability across diverse communication styles and cultures.
*   Emotional authenticity remains an open challenge as AI is not capable of the complex nuance in emotional expression and perception.

*Potential Influence:* The paper has the potential to influence the design and implementation of AI-driven simulation tools in healthcare education. It provides valuable guidance for creating more effective and emotionally sensitive training programs for palliative care and other domains requiring complex communication skills. The emphasis on hybrid training models, combining AI with human mentorship, aligns with a pragmatic approach to integrating technology into medical education. It can encourage further research on adaptive feedback mechanisms and personalized learning pathways in medical simulation.

*Overall:* The paper makes a valuable contribution to the intersection of HCI, medical education, and AI. It identifies a critical need, presents a practical solution, and provides empirical evidence to support its effectiveness. While there are limitations, the findings offer valuable insights for improving communication training in palliative care and other high-stakes clinical settings.

### Generative Latent Diffusion for Efficient Spatiotemporal Data Reduction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02129v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Li, Liangji Zhu, Anand Rangarajan, Sanjay Ranka
- **Abstract**: Generative models have demonstrated strong performance in conditional settings and can be viewed as a form of data compression, where the condition serves as a compact representation. However, their limited controllability and reconstruction accuracy restrict their practical application to data compression. In this work, we propose an efficient latent diffusion framework that bridges this gap by combining a variational autoencoder with a conditional diffusion model. Our method compresses only a small number of keyframes into latent space and uses them as conditioning inputs to reconstruct the remaining frames via generative interpolation, eliminating the need to store latent representations for every frame. This approach enables accurate spatiotemporal reconstruction while significantly reducing storage costs. Experimental results across multiple datasets show that our method achieves up to 10 times higher compression ratios than rule-based state-of-the-art compressors such as SZ3, and up to 63 percent better performance than leading learning-based methods under the same reconstruction error.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Generative Latent Diffusion for Efficient Spatiotemporal Data Reduction" and will provide a summary and a critical evaluation with a score.

**Summary**

The paper proposes a novel data compression framework specifically designed for spatiotemporal scientific data. This framework combines a variational autoencoder (VAE) with a conditional diffusion model to achieve higher compression ratios while maintaining reconstruction accuracy and guaranteeing error bounds. The key idea is to compress only a small set of keyframes into latent space using the VAE, and then use the conditional diffusion model to generate (interpolate) the remaining frames based on the keyframe latent representations. A post-processing step, based on PCA, ensures that user-defined error bounds are met.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method on three scientific datasets (E3SM, S3D, and JHTDB), showing significant improvements in compression ratio compared to both traditional (rule-based) methods like SZ3 and ZFP, and learning-based methods like VAE-SR.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of using generative models, specifically conditional diffusion models, for *interpolation* rather than direct synthesis in data compression is relatively novel and has significant promise.  Repurposing diffusion models as learned, nonlinear interpolators is an innovative approach. This addresses a key limitation of other compression techniques: the requirement to store latent representations for *every* frame or block.
*   **Significant Performance Improvement:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate the superior performance of the proposed method compared to state-of-the-art compression techniques. Achieving 4-10x higher compression ratios than rule-based methods and 20-63% improvement over leading learning-based methods at the same reconstruction error is substantial.
*   **Error Bound Guarantee:** The inclusion of a post-processing step to guarantee error bounds is crucial for the adoption of lossy compression in scientific applications. The use of PCA for this purpose, while not entirely novel, is a practical and effective solution.
*   **Relevance to Scientific Community:** The problem of efficiently storing and processing massive scientific datasets is highly relevant, and this work directly addresses a critical need within the scientific computing community.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conducted thorough experiments on a diverse set of scientific datasets, providing convincing evidence of the generalizability and effectiveness of their method. The ablation studies (e.g., varying keyframe intervals, denoising steps) are also valuable.
*   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-organized and clearly explains the proposed method and the experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **PCA Post-processing Overhead:** While the PCA-based error correction step is essential for error bound guarantees, the paper mentions that this step is currently implemented on the CPU and slower than GPU-based components. This limits the overall end-to-end performance and warrants future GPU optimization.
*   **Domain-Specific Hyperparameter Tuning:** The optimal keyframe interval is shown to vary depending on the dataset. While this is not a fatal flaw, it suggests that some domain-specific tuning may be required for optimal performance, potentially hindering the "out-of-the-box" usability of the method.
*   **Complexity:** Compared to traditional methods like ZFP or SZ, this approach is significantly more complex. While the benefits in compression ratio are substantial, the increased computational cost and implementation complexity may be a barrier to adoption in some cases. The paper would benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational cost and resource requirements (e.g., memory footprint) of the method.
*   **Limited Novelty in Error Bounding:**  While the overall framework is novel, the PCA-based error bounding method is a well-established technique, already used in other scientific compression studies.
*   **Lack of Comparison on QoI Preservation**: While error bounds on PD are guaranteed, it would be stronger if the paper also provided evidence (or guarantees) on the preservation of QoI (Quantities of Interest). The introduction mentions the importance of QoI but does not discuss it further.
*   **Unclear choice of Keyframes:** The current method chooses keyframes at regular intervals. While an exploration, the paper does not explain why this technique is superior to other choices of keyframes.
*   **No results for other compression methods:** While the paper shows results for methods that have been used for compression purposes, it would add rigor if some comparison to commonly used video compression methods was used as video streams may be considered to be a similar problem space.
*   **Missing Implementation details:** It is unclear how the diffusion model is conditioned on the keyframe representations in the architecture.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of scientific data compression. The proposed method offers a compelling trade-off between compression ratio, reconstruction accuracy, and error bound guarantees. The use of conditional diffusion for latent space interpolation is a novel and promising approach that has the potential to significantly impact how scientific data is stored and processed. The method is very practical given the extensive experimental validation with real-world datasets.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5. The paper has several strengths: novelty in applying diffusion models for *interpolation* in a compression context, substantial performance improvements over existing methods, inclusion of error bound guarantees, and relevance to a critical need in the scientific community. It improves upon existing diffusion-based approaches to compression by significantly reducing the number of latent vectors that need to be stored, leading to higher compression ratios. However, it also has weaknesses: the reliance on the already established PCA post-processing component, domain-specific hyperparameter tuning needed, the complexity and CPU based post processing, and missing discussion/evidence of QoI preservation and the lack of results for alternative compression techniques, choice of keyframe intervals, and missing architecture details.  Given the paper's strengths relative to its weaknesses, it presents a significant advancement with the potential to be highly influential, thus justifying a good score in the above range.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dissecting the Impact of Mobile DVFS Governors on LLM Inference Performance and Energy Efficiency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02135v1)
- **Authors**: Zongpu Zhang, Pranab Dash, Y. Charlie Hu, Qiang Xu, Jian Li, Haibing Guan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into various applications and services running on billions of mobile devices. However, deploying LLMs on resource-limited mobile devices faces a significant challenge due to their high demand for computation, memory, and ultimately energy. While current LLM frameworks for mobile use three power-hungry components-CPU, GPU, and Memory-even when running primarily-GPU LLM models, optimized DVFS governors for CPU, GPU, and memory featured in modern mobile devices operate independently and are oblivious of each other. Motivated by the above observation, in this work, we first measure the energy-efficiency of a SOTA LLM framework consisting of various LLM models on mobile phones which showed the triplet mobile governors result in up to 40.4% longer prefilling and decoding latency compared to optimal combinations of CPU, GPU, and memory frequencies with the same energy consumption for sampled prefill and decode lengths. Second, we conduct an in-depth measurement study to uncover how the intricate interplay (or lack of) among the mobile governors cause the above inefficiency in LLM inference. Finally, based on these insights, we design FUSE - a unified energy-aware governor for optimizing the energy efficiency of LLM inference on mobile devices. Our evaluation using a ShareGPT dataset shows FUSE reduces the time-to-first-token and time-per-output-token latencies by 7.0%-16.9% and 25.4%-36.8% on average with the same energy-per-token for various mobile LLM models.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper investigates the impact of mobile Dynamic Voltage and Frequency Scaling (DVFS) governors on the performance and energy efficiency of Large Language Model (LLM) inference on mobile devices. The authors find that the standard Android governors for CPU, GPU, and memory, which operate independently, can lead to suboptimal performance. They demonstrate that these governors can trigger an antagonistic effect where each component scales down its frequency, resulting in longer latencies and higher energy consumption. To address this, the authors propose FUSE, a unified energy-aware governor that optimizes the frequency combination for CPU, GPU, and memory based on either an energy budget or a latency target. Evaluations on a variety of LLM models show that FUSE can reduce latency by 7.0%-16.9% and 25.4%-36.8% on average, while maintaining the same energy consumption, relative to default governors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has decent novelty. While prior work has explored energy efficiency in LLM inference on mobile devices, this paper is arguably the first to deeply analyze the intricate interplay of standard Android DVFS governors and their individual contributions to the problem. Identifying the antagonistic effect is a key insight that hasn't been explored before. The proposed FUSE governor builds upon this understanding, but unified governor design is a reasonably explored space and thus its individual novelty is less compelling. The analysis of individual governor contribution is of limited novelty, but helps support the main contribution.

**Significance:**

The findings are practically significant. LLMs on mobile devices are becoming increasingly common, and improving their energy efficiency is critical for battery life and user experience. By exposing the limitations of existing DVFS governors, the authors have highlighted a clear opportunity for optimization. Furthermore, the methodology of controlled experiments and detailed power/performance measurements is useful for future researchers. FUSE demonstrates significant improvement across multiple models which points to good generalization capabilities.

**Strengths:**

*   **In-depth Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed and systematic analysis of the DVFS governor interplay, using controlled experiments to isolate individual and combined effects.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and defines the problem of suboptimal governor behavior for LLM inference.
*   **Practical Solution:** The proposed FUSE governor is a practical solution that achieves significant performance improvements.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation uses a variety of LLM models and a real-world dataset (ShareGPT) to demonstrate the effectiveness of FUSE.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Unified Governor Novelty:** The novelty of the unified governor design is limited. FUSE relies on offline profiling for frequency selection.
*   **Limited Generalization (to other devices):** The evaluation is limited to two Google Pixel devices. It would be important to see whether the same effects happen with other device vendors.
*   **Practical applicability of the FUSE method is limited by the need of root privileges on the device, and access to low level governor control.**
*   **Limited Exploration of memory governor:** The paper claims the memory governor can achieve near optimal behavior, and excludes it from the FUSE configuration process. This exclusion simplifies the problem, but isn't necessarily correct. It would be more compelling to see a full analysis of that decision, which could potentially limit the benefits of the proposed approach.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's primary contribution is uncovering the antagonistic interaction between mobile DVFS governors during LLM inference, and using that insight to design a unified governor. The study provides actionable insights by showing that the default configurations of mobile OS's are not adequate for optimal performance. While the unified governor design itself has limited individual novelty, the combination of the insights from experiments and the demonstrated latency reduction in diverse settings gives it good significance. FUSE presents a useful contribution given the rising integration of LLMs in on-device mobile applications.
However the overall novelty is limited by prior art, its device dependance and the complexity involved in deploying it (rooted devices).

### When LLMs Disagree: Diagnosing Relevance Filtering Bias and Retrieval Divergence in SDG Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02139v1)
- **Authors**: William A. Ingram, Bipasha Banerjee, Edward A. Fox
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to assign document relevance labels in information retrieval pipelines, especially in domains lacking human-labeled data. However, different models often disagree on borderline cases, raising concerns about how such disagreement affects downstream retrieval. This study examines labeling disagreement between two open-weight LLMs, LLaMA and Qwen, on a corpus of scholarly abstracts related to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 1, 3, and 7. We isolate disagreement subsets and examine their lexical properties, rank-order behavior, and classification predictability. Our results show that model disagreement is systematic, not random: disagreement cases exhibit consistent lexical patterns, produce divergent top-ranked outputs under shared scoring functions, and are distinguishable with AUCs above 0.74 using simple classifiers. These findings suggest that LLM-based filtering introduces structured variability in document retrieval, even under controlled prompting and shared ranking logic. We propose using classification disagreement as an object of analysis in retrieval evaluation, particularly in policy-relevant or thematic search tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of disagreement between two open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs), LLaMA and Qwen, when used to filter scholarly abstracts for relevance to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The authors isolate disagreement subsets and analyze their lexical properties, rank-order behavior under shared scoring functions, and classification predictability. Their findings indicate that LLM disagreement is systematic, not random, and reveals model-specific filtering preferences. These filtering decisions subsequently lead to divergent retrieval profiles, even with shared ranking logic, highlighting how automated filtering can reshape SDG-based retrieval in the absence of a human-labeled ground truth.  The paper explores whether systematic differences exist in what each model filters in or out, and examines the implications of these differences for ranked retrieval. It also addresses the learnability of filtering differences between the models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on analyzing and diagnosing *disagreement* between LLMs as a signal of structured variability in document retrieval, rather than treating it simply as noise to be averaged out.  While previous work has acknowledged LLM disagreement, this paper delves deeper into its causes and consequences within a specific, policy-relevant domain (SDGs). The specific application of this analysis to SDG-related literature and bibliometric retrieval adds further value. The use of lexical analysis, TF-IDF techniques to demonstrate and explain differences in relevance judgments is a plus.
* **Significance:** The work addresses a critical challenge in IR, especially in domains where labeled data is scarce and human adjudication is infeasible. LLMs are increasingly used for relevance labeling, and understanding how their inherent variability affects downstream retrieval is crucial. Demonstrating that even with controlled prompting and shared ranking logic, LLM-based filtering introduces systematic biases is a significant finding. The implications of these biases are considerable, potentially affecting access to evidence in policy-relevant tasks. The paper shows that models' judgments are learnable from surface features alone, supporting the predictability of model behavior. It effectively highlights the necessity of retrieval-aware evaluation of LLM filtering, particularly in high-stakes contexts. The methodology, which combines quantitative analysis (agreement statistics, TF-IDF, AUC) with qualitative examination of retrieved examples, is well-suited for the research questions.
* **Strengths:**
    *  Clear research questions and well-defined methodology.
    *  Rigorous analysis of the disagreement subsets, including statistical significance testing.
    *  Demonstration of how filtering decisions affect downstream ranking.
    *  Policy relevance through its focus on SDG retrieval.
    *  The use of two open-source models enables reproducibility.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The study is limited to two LLMs (LLaMA and Qwen) and three SDGs, which restricts the generalizability of the findings. While the chosen SDGs are important, expanding the scope could strengthen the conclusions.
    * The evaluation relies on a shared ranking function, which may not fully reflect real-world retrieval scenarios. Exploring different ranking models could provide a more comprehensive understanding of the impact of LLM filtering.
    * The paper mainly focuses on lexical and term-based analysis. Future work could incorporate contextualized embeddings for better capturing semantic differences.
    * The conclusions are somewhat limited by the lack of human gold standard relevance labels to further compare the models against.

* **Impact and Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research on LLM-based IR, particularly in areas related to policy and thematic search. It encourages researchers to consider the variability of LLM judgments not just as noise, but as a valuable source of information about model behavior. It provides a concrete methodology for diagnosing filtering biases and analyzing their impact on retrieval.  It also sets a good foundation for ensemble or combined filtering or ranking approaches as a countermeasure for potentially inherent model-related biases.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of IR by focusing on analyzing and diagnosing the effects of LLM disagreement in relevance filtering. While limited in scope (number of models and SDGs), the study is rigorous, well-structured, and provides compelling evidence of systematic filtering biases. The methodological soundness and potential for real-world impact justify a score of 7.  A higher score would be warranted with a broader model coverage and demonstration of improved downstream task performance or use cases with gold standard relevance datasets.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning or Not? A Comprehensive Evaluation of Reasoning LLMs for Dialogue Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02145v1)
- **Authors**: Keyan Jin, Yapeng Wang, Leonel Santos, Tao Fang, Xu Yang, Sio Kei Im, Hugo Gonçalo Oliveira
- **Abstract**: Dialogue summarization is a challenging task with significant practical value in customer service, meeting analysis, and conversational AI. Although large language models (LLMs) have achieved substantial progress in summarization tasks, the performance of step-by-step reasoning architectures-specifically Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) implementations such as OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1-remains unexplored for dialogue scenarios requiring concurrent abstraction and conciseness. In this work, we present the first comprehensive and systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs and non-reasoning LLMs across three major paradigms-generic, role-oriented, and query-oriented dialogue summarization. Our study spans diverse languages, domains, and summary lengths, leveraging strong benchmarks (SAMSum, DialogSum, CSDS, and QMSum) and advanced evaluation protocols that include both LLM-based automatic metrics and human-inspired criteria. Contrary to trends in other reasoning-intensive tasks, our findings show that explicit stepwise reasoning does not consistently improve dialogue summarization quality. Instead, reasoning LLMs are often prone to verbosity, factual inconsistencies, and less concise summaries compared to their non-reasoning counterparts. Through scenario-specific analyses and detailed case studies, we further identify when and why explicit reasoning may fail to benefit-or even hinder-summarization in complex dialogue contexts. Our work provides new insights into the limitations of current reasoning LLMs and highlights the need for targeted modeling and evaluation strategies for real-world dialogue summarization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of reasoning Large Language Models (LLMs) for dialogue summarization. It investigates whether the "chain-of-thought" approach, which is generally beneficial in reasoning-intensive tasks, consistently improves summarization quality in dialogue contexts. The study evaluates both reasoning and non-reasoning LLMs across three summarization paradigms (generic, role-oriented, and query-oriented) using various datasets, languages, and summary lengths.  The authors find that explicit stepwise reasoning does not consistently improve dialogue summarization quality. Reasoning LLMs are often more verbose, factually inconsistent, and less concise compared to their non-reasoning counterparts.  The authors identify scenarios where explicit reasoning may hinder summarization and provide insights into the limitations of current reasoning LLMs for real-world dialogue summarization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic and comprehensive comparison of reasoning LLMs against traditional LLMs specifically within the context of dialogue summarization.  While other papers have examined LLMs for general summarization or specific reasoning tasks, this paper carves out a specific niche and provides valuable insights that run contrary to the expected benefits of reasoning in this task. The finding that reasoning LLMs *underperform* in some dialogue summarization scenarios is significant and challenges the assumption that simply applying reasoning capabilities will automatically lead to better performance.
* **Significance:** This research has significant implications for the development and deployment of LLMs in real-world applications involving dialogue summarization. The finding that reasoning models don't always outperform non-reasoning ones prompts a reevaluation of model selection and prompt engineering strategies. It emphasizes the need for more targeted modeling and evaluation strategies, recognizing the specific demands of dialogue (brevity, factual accuracy, etc.).
* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors perform a rigorous evaluation covering a wide range of models, datasets, and evaluation metrics. This comprehensive approach strengthens the validity and generalizability of their findings. The use of both automatic and LLM-as-a-judge metrics provides a multi-faceted view of summary quality.
    * **Scenario-Driven Analysis:** The scenario-specific analysis allows for a nuanced understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of different models under different conditions. This is more valuable than a simple aggregate performance comparison.
    * **Detailed Case Studies:** The inclusion of case studies helps to illustrate the specific failure modes of reasoning LLMs, providing concrete examples of how verbosity and factual inconsistencies can arise.
    * **Clear Methodology:** The paper's methodology is well-defined, making it easy to understand and reproduce the experiments. The prompt design and experimental setup are carefully considered.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Focus on Specific Reasoning Strategies:** The paper generally lumps all "reasoning LLMs" together. It might be useful to dissect *specific* reasoning strategies (e.g., causal reasoning, abductive reasoning) and how each one impacts performance.  This would provide a more granular understanding.
    * **Dependency on LLM Evaluators:** While LLM-based evaluation is becoming more common, it is important to acknowledge its limitations.  The paper touches upon this (difficulties in evaluating factuality). A reliance on LLM evaluators might introduce biases or limitations that are not fully addressed.
    * **Limited Scope of Datasets:**  While a good range, the datasets used are still limited in domain. Are these findings generalizable to very specific domains (e.g., medical dialogues)?
    * **Lack of prescriptive solutions:** The paper clearly points out the issues but offers no specific mechanisms or methods to remedy these situations
* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings are likely to influence future research on LLMs for dialogue summarization. It will encourage researchers to move beyond simply scaling up models and to focus on more targeted strategies that address the specific challenges of this task. It also highlights the importance of developing more robust and reliable evaluation metrics for assessing summarization quality.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is valuable and makes a significant contribution by challenging assumptions about the universal benefits of reasoning in LLMs, specifically within the context of dialogue summarization. The comprehensive nature of the experiments and the clear articulation of the results are commendable.  The key limitations are the dependency on current LLM-based evaluators, the scope of the dataset covered and a lack of prescriptive guidelines. The paper pushes the field forward and opens the door for much-needed focus on more targeted modeling and evaluation strategies.
