- **Authors**: Wenzhe Guo, Joyjit Kundu, Uras Tos, Weijiang Kong, Giuliano Sisto, Timon Evenblij, Manu Perumkunnil
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs), based on transformer architectures, have revolutionized numerous domains within artificial intelligence, science, and engineering due to their exceptional scalability and adaptability. However, the exponential growth in LLM size and complexity has outpaced advancements in compute capacity, memory bandwidth, network performance, and cost efficiency, posing significant challenges to their scalability on distributed systems. To address these limitations, alternative model architectures, optimization strategies, communication-aware network topologies, and novel system design approaches have been proposed in literature. This paper introduces a performance-cost modeling methodology for LLM training and inference that integrates state-of-the-art compute techniques with memory optimizations, and latest communication techniques. Building on an analytical performance model, our approach incorporates recent innovations such as the flash attention technique and mixture of experts models to address the memory bandwidth and compute bottlenecks. It also considers the impact of different network topologies and topology-specific communication algorithms with 5D parallellism. The framework also integrates a chiplet cost model. The proposed modeling methodology provides valuable insights to guide future compute system design and facilitates hardware-software co-development, in particular due to its ability to analyze performance-cost trade-offs for various system architectural configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a performance and cost modeling methodology for Large Language Model (LLM) training and inference.  The work builds upon the Optimus framework, incorporating advancements like FlashAttention, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and topology-aware communication algorithms, considering a 5D parallelism approach. The authors integrate a chiplet-based cost model with the performance model to allow a unified, automated framework for exploring performance-cost trade-offs in LLM system design. The paper includes validation using published data and case studies illustrating the framework's capabilities.

**Rigorous Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in integrating multiple advanced techniques into a single modeling framework and including a cost model. While individual components like FlashAttention and MoE are well-established, their combined modeling within a distributed LLM environment, alongside network topology optimizations *and* cost considerations, is a relatively novel contribution. Expanding the design space exploration to 5D parallelism, by including expert parallelism, is a plus. However, most of these components are based on existing techniques; the primary contribution is their integration and the modeling framework's extension to incorporate them.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing need for efficient and cost-effective LLM deployments. Performance modeling *and* cost estimations are crucial for guiding hardware-software co-design in this space. The ability to evaluate different architectural configurations and optimization strategies *without* requiring actual prototyping is valuable. The inclusion of a chiplet-based cost model is particularly relevant, given the growing use of chiplets in high-performance computing.  The paper addresses a practical problem with a comprehensive approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive Modeling: The framework integrates several key aspects relevant to LLM performance, including compute, memory, communication, parallelism strategies, and cost.
    *   Practical Focus: The work addresses a real-world problem in LLM deployment: balancing performance and cost.
    *   Validation: The validation against published data lends credibility to the modeling approach.
    *   Case Studies: The case studies demonstrate the framework's ability to provide insights for system design decisions.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Dependence on Existing Techniques: While the integration is valuable, the core techniques (FlashAttention, MoE, parallelization strategies) are borrowed from prior work. This reduces the novelty to an architectural level.
    *   Cost Model Simplifications: The cost model, while present, might oversimplify the complexities of chiplet manufacturing and integration.  The level of detail in cost modeling compared to performance modeling is a potential imbalance. For example, supply chain aspects, design complexity dependent yield, and advanced packaging specifics are likely not comprehensively captured.
    *   Limited Scope: The cost model focuses mainly on chiplets and does not include data center costs, such as cooling, infrastructure, and power. This leaves the cost model incomplete from a TCO perspective.
    *   Limited Depth of Validation: The validation could be expanded with a more extensive evaluation against real-world system data, particularly in the interaction between performance and cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The framework has the potential to influence the design of future compute systems for LLMs by enabling early-stage performance and cost trade-off analysis. The work is directly applicable to hardware and software engineers working on LLM deployments. However, the framework's complexity and reliance on specific modeling techniques could limit its wider adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by integrating diverse LLM optimization techniques and cost modeling into a single framework. This aids hardware-software co-design and accelerates the exploration of potential design solutions.  However, the primary contribution is integration rather than invention. The strengths are well-executed implementation and validation and the potential impact is considerable in the current climate of LLM deployment.

Given the reasonably high potential influence but some shortcomings in novelty and areas of oversimplification, I believe a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### MedFormer: Hierarchical Medical Vision Transformer with Content-Aware Dual Sparse Selection Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02488v1)
- **Authors**: Zunhui Xia, Hongxing Li, Libin Lan
- **Abstract**: Medical image recognition serves as a key way to aid in clinical diagnosis, enabling more accurate and timely identification of diseases and abnormalities. Vision transformer-based approaches have proven effective in handling various medical recognition tasks. However, these methods encounter two primary challenges. First, they are often task-specific and architecture-tailored, limiting their general applicability. Second, they usually either adopt full attention to model long-range dependencies, resulting in high computational costs, or rely on handcrafted sparse attention, potentially leading to suboptimal performance. To tackle these issues, we present MedFormer, an efficient medical vision transformer with two key ideas. First, it employs a pyramid scaling structure as a versatile backbone for various medical image recognition tasks, including image classification and dense prediction tasks such as semantic segmentation and lesion detection. This structure facilitates hierarchical feature representation while reducing the computation load of feature maps, highly beneficial for boosting performance. Second, it introduces a novel Dual Sparse Selection Attention (DSSA) with content awareness to improve computational efficiency and robustness against noise while maintaining high performance. As the core building technique of MedFormer, DSSA is explicitly designed to attend to the most relevant content. In addition, a detailed theoretical analysis has been conducted, demonstrating that MedFormer has superior generality and efficiency in comparison to existing medical vision transformers. Extensive experiments on a variety of imaging modality datasets consistently show that MedFormer is highly effective in enhancing performance across all three above-mentioned medical image recognition tasks. The code is available at https://github.com/XiaZunhui/MedFormer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MedFormer: Hierarchical Medical Vision Transformer with Content-Aware Dual Sparse Selection Attention":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MedFormer, a new vision transformer architecture designed for medical image recognition tasks. MedFormer aims to be a general-purpose backbone, addressing limitations of existing task-specific medical vision transformers. It incorporates two key ideas:

1.  **Pyramid Scaling Structure:**  A hierarchical structure generates multi-scale feature representations, reducing computational cost and enriching semantic information suitable for various tasks like classification, segmentation, and detection.
2.  **Dual Sparse Selection Attention (DSSA):** A novel attention mechanism that selects the most relevant content in two steps. First, it filters out irrelevant regions, and then it performs pixel-level sparse selection within these regions, reducing computational complexity and improving robustness against noise.

The authors conduct extensive experiments on several medical imaging datasets, demonstrating that MedFormer enhances performance across classification, segmentation, and detection tasks. They also provide theoretical analysis and ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Generalizability:** The design aims to create a truly general-purpose backbone, a welcome departure from the task-specific architectures that dominate the field. The hierarchical structure is well-suited for diverse tasks.
    *   **Efficient Attention (DSSA):** The DSSA mechanism is a clever way to reduce computational cost without sacrificing performance. The two-step sparse selection is theoretically justified and empirically validated as a strategy for improving efficiency and robustness.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough and cover a variety of tasks and datasets. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of MedFormer.
    *   **Strong Results:** The empirical results consistently show that MedFormer achieves competitive performance compared to existing methods.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, and the architecture and the proposed mechanisms are well-explained.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of ideas in MedFormer is effective, the individual components (pyramid structure, sparse attention) are not entirely novel. The main novelty lies in the specific implementation of DSSA within a hierarchical transformer designed for medical imaging. The innovation, while practical, might be considered incremental rather than ground-breaking.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** While the authors perform ablation studies, there is still a high number of hyperparameters in the method (e.g. how to choose `k1` and `k2`). A more comprehensive hyperparameter optimization process or guidelines for setting these parameters would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited comparison datasets on detection task**: Only one dataset is for brain tumour and only one for Kvasir-Seg. There are many detection dataset for medical use cases which would have strenghtened the claims for generalization.
    *   **Lack of detailed ablation for all tasks**: Some of the ablation studies, whilst insightful, focus only on the classification task. It is not clear if the parameters for DSSA optimized for a single tasks transfer well to other tasks like segmentation and detection.

*   **Significance:**

The paper addresses a relevant challenge in the medical image analysis field: the need for more general-purpose, efficient, and robust vision transformers. The MedFormer architecture and the DSSA mechanism offer a promising approach to address this challenge. While the novelty of individual components might be incremental, the specific design choices and the thorough evaluation contribute a valuable addition to the field. MedFormer has the potential to become a useful backbone for medical image recognition, facilitating research and application in diverse clinical scenarios. The code availability will also encourage further adoption and development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of medical image analysis by presenting a general-purpose vision transformer backbone with an efficient attention mechanism. However, the novelty lies primarily in the specific integration and adaptation of existing concepts, rather than a completely disruptive innovation. The detailed evaluation and strong results support the claims, but some parameter sensitivity remains a concern. Although a variety of ablation studies are conducted, the scope of the ablated tasks is still limited. The paper's potential impact is further limited by the presence of other general-purpose backbone architectures in the computer vision community. Therefore, while the paper is valuable, a higher score would necessitate a more substantial breakthrough in fundamental concepts.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Continual Gradient Low-Rank Projection Fine-Tuning for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02503v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxu Wang, Yilin Lyu, Zicheng Sun, Liping Jing
- **Abstract**: Continual fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) is hampered by the trade-off between efficiency and expressiveness. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) offers efficiency but constrains the model's ability to learn new tasks and transfer knowledge due to its low-rank nature and reliance on explicit parameter constraints. We propose GORP (Gradient LOw Rank Projection) for Continual Learning, a novel training strategy that overcomes these limitations by synergistically combining full and low-rank parameters and jointly updating within a unified low-rank gradient subspace. GORP expands the optimization space while preserving efficiency and mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Extensive experiments on continual learning benchmarks demonstrate GORP's superior performance compared to existing state-of-the-art approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/Wcxwcxw/GORP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel training strategy called GORP (Gradient Low-Rank Projection) for continual fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs). GORP addresses the trade-off between efficiency and expressiveness in continual learning by synergistically combining full-rank and low-rank parameter updates within a unified low-rank gradient subspace. The method leverages the observation that gradients tend to adopt a low-rank structure during training, projecting full-rank gradients into a low-rank space to maintain efficiency and expand the search space for optimal solutions. GORP uses the first-order moment of gradients to implicitly capture the dynamic properties of the gradient space, mitigating catastrophic forgetting. The paper presents extensive experiments on continual learning benchmarks, demonstrating superior performance compared to existing state-of-the-art approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the synergistic combination of full-rank and low-rank updates *within* a low-rank gradient subspace, guided by the first-order moment of gradients.  Prior work often focuses on either full-rank or low-rank adaptation, or on explicit constraints.  GORP's integrated approach and use of gradient momentum for subspace construction seem like a solid addition. While gradient projection itself isn't entirely new in continual learning, the specific implementation within a low-rank adaptation setting and the use of gradient momentum for shaping the subspace adds novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the performance and adaptability of LLMs in continual learning scenarios, a crucial area for real-world applications where models need to learn new tasks without forgetting previously acquired knowledge. Demonstrating consistent improvement over existing methods on established benchmarks increases the paper's impact. The ablation studies are also important for validating the design choices.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained method with supporting figures and pseudo-code.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple datasets and models.
    *   Ablation studies to validate design choices.
    *   Comparison against strong baselines.
    *   Analyses of parameter and gradient orthogonality.
    *   Consideration of time complexity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While well-motivated, the assumption that gradients primarily live in low-rank spaces requires further theoretical or empirical justification. Perhaps the authors could investigate the singular value spectrum of the gradients over time, showing how quickly it decays.
    *   The dependence on Adam and its first-order moments may introduce biases. It might be beneficial to explore other optimizers or gradient estimation techniques to see if the results generalize.
    *   The experimental setting could be expanded to include more complex, real-world continual learning scenarios. Also, the size difference between the two models utilized raises concerns. The result of T5-large is not transferable to LLaMa2 7B, and therefore should be emphasized more in discussion.
    *   The analysis of time complexity is somewhat shallow. A more detailed analysis of the computational cost of the different components of GORP would be useful.
    *   While the method outperforms baselines, the performance gap relative to MTL is still significant, indicating room for further improvement.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the results hold up in more diverse and challenging scenarios, GORP could influence future research in parameter-efficient continual learning for LLMs. The integrated approach could inspire new methods that combine the benefits of full-rank and low-rank adaptation.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a well-executed and promising approach, a few limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty, though present, builds upon existing concepts like low-rank adaptation and gradient projection. The improvements are significant on the chosen benchmarks, but questions remain about its scalability to larger models and robustness in more realistic scenarios, and the size variance between the two models raises concerns.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Meta-Fair: AI-Assisted Fairness Testing of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02533v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Romero-Arjona, José A. Parejo, Juan C. Alonso, Ana B. Sánchez, Aitor Arrieta, Sergio Segura
- **Abstract**: Fairness--the absence of unjustified bias--is a core principle in the development of Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems, yet it remains difficult to assess and enforce. Current approaches to fairness testing in large language models (LLMs) often rely on manual evaluation, fixed templates, deterministic heuristics, and curated datasets, making them resource-intensive and difficult to scale. This work aims to lay the groundwork for a novel, automated method for testing fairness in LLMs, reducing the dependence on domain-specific resources and broadening the applicability of current approaches. Our approach, Meta-Fair, is based on two key ideas. First, we adopt metamorphic testing to uncover bias by examining how model outputs vary in response to controlled modifications of input prompts, defined by metamorphic relations (MRs). Second, we propose exploiting the potential of LLMs for both test case generation and output evaluation, leveraging their capability to generate diverse inputs and classify outputs effectively. The proposal is complemented by three open-source tools supporting LLM-driven generation, execution, and evaluation of test cases. We report the findings of several experiments involving 12 pre-trained LLMs, 14 MRs, 5 bias dimensions, and 7.9K automatically generated test cases. The results show that Meta-Fair is effective in uncovering bias in LLMs, achieving an average precision of 92% and revealing biased behaviour in 29% of executions. Additionally, LLMs prove to be reliable and consistent evaluators, with the best-performing models achieving F1-scores of up to 0.79. Although non-determinism affects consistency, these effects can be mitigated through careful MR design. While challenges remain to ensure broader applicability, the results indicate a promising path towards an unprecedented level of automation in LLM testing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Meta-Fair: AI-Assisted Fairness Testing of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Meta-Fair, a novel approach to automated fairness testing of Large Language Models (LLMs). It combines metamorphic testing, where input prompts are modified based on metamorphic relations (MRs), with the use of LLMs for both test case generation and output evaluation. Meta-Fair includes three open-source tools for generation, execution, and evaluation of test cases. The authors conduct experiments on 12 pre-trained LLMs using 14 MRs across 5 bias dimensions, and report that the approach is effective in uncovering bias. LLMs are shown to be reliable evaluators, but non-determinism can affect consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of metamorphic testing with LLM-driven test generation and evaluation is a significant contribution. While metamorphic testing has been applied to LLMs before, leveraging LLMs *themselves* to generate test cases and act as judges is a novel and scalable idea. The introduction of 13 new MRs (though based on variations of existing metamorphic principles) tailored to LLMs also adds value.  The claim that this is the *first* approach integrating metamorphic testing and LLMs for test case generation and evaluation is well-supported, at least within the paper's specific focus on fairness.  Previous work leaned more on structured tasks/datasets or approximate oracles.

*   **Significance:** Fairness in AI is a crucial area, and the ability to automatically test for bias is highly valuable. Meta-Fair addresses the scalability challenges of traditional fairness testing methods by automating the process. The open-source tools accompanying the paper make the approach more accessible and reproducible.  The results demonstrating effectiveness (average precision of 92%) support the claim of a promising path towards unprecedented automation. The experiments cover a variety of models, MRs, and bias dimensions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical need: Automated fairness testing.
    *   Novel combination of metamorphic testing and LLMs as generators/evaluators.
    *   Open-source tools enhance accessibility and reproducibility.
    *   Demonstrates effectiveness through experiments.
    *   Clearly defines MRs, templates, and evaluation metrics.
    *   Acknowledges and tries to mitigate non-determinism (although this remains a weakness).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Non-determinism still affects consistency, which could lead to false positives or negatives. More robust strategies for dealing with this are needed.  While the paper mentions careful MR design as a mitigating factor, more concrete guidelines are needed.
    *   While 12 LLMs were tested as models under test, the generation and evaluation relied heavily on GPT-4o mini and Llama 3.3.  Broader exploration here would strengthen generalizability.
    *   Certain MRs (e.g., those based on implicit cues or closed-ended questions) had limitations in effectiveness.  This highlights the difficulty in designing universally effective MRs and potentially limits the scope of applicability.
    *   The human evaluation, though performed with strong inter-annotator agreement, introduces a level of subjectivity that could influence the ground truth dataset.
    *   Focus is on black-box testing.  While this is practical, insights from white-box investigations could further improve fairness testing methods.
    *   Lack of comparison to other state-of-the-art fairness testing approaches for LLMs. It's difficult to assess the superiority of Meta-Fair without a direct comparison.

*   **Impact:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in automated fairness testing for LLMs. The Meta-Fair approach offers a more scalable and automated method than existing approaches and can be used for initial testing and detection of biases. The approach also provides a foundation upon which other fairness tools can be built.

**Justification of Score:**

Meta-Fair presents a novel approach with clear practical significance for fairness testing. The weaknesses related to non-determinism, limitations in MR design and lack of comparison to other methods hold back the paper from a higher score. The open-source implementation and thorough experimentation are major strengths. The paper clearly lays the groundwork for future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are You Listening to Me? Fine-Tuning Chatbots for Empathetic Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02537v1)
- **Authors**: Paulo Ricardo Knob, Leonardo Scholler, Juliano Rigatti, Soraia Raupp Musse
- **Abstract**: Conversational agents have made significant progress since ELIZA, expanding their role across various domains, including healthcare, education, and customer service. As these agents become increasingly integrated into daily human interactions, the need for emotional intelligence, particularly empathetic listening, becomes increasingly essential. In this study, we explore how Large Language Models (LLMs) respond when tasked with generating emotionally rich interactions. Starting from a small dataset manually crafted by an expert to reflect empathic behavior, we extended the conversations using two LLMs: ChatGPT and Gemini. We analyzed the emotional progression of the dialogues using both sentiment analysis (via VADER) and expert assessments. While the generated conversations often mirrored the intended emotional structure, human evaluation revealed important differences in the perceived empathy and coherence of the responses. These findings suggest that emotion modeling in dialogues requires not only structural alignment in the expressed emotions but also qualitative depth, highlighting the importance of combining automated and humancentered methods in the development of emotionally competent agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) for empathetic listening in conversational agents. The authors created a small dataset of expert-authored empathetic dialogues and extended these using ChatGPT and Gemini. They analyzed the emotional progression of the dialogues using VADER sentiment analysis and expert assessments. While LLM-generated conversations often showed a structural increase in emotional energy, human evaluation revealed shortcomings in genuine empathy and coherence compared to the expert-authored examples. The study highlights the need for qualitative depth beyond structural emotional alignment when modeling empathy in dialogues and emphasizes the importance of human-centered methods in developing emotionally competent agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles an important and timely problem: enhancing emotional intelligence, particularly empathetic listening, in conversational AI. As AI increasingly interacts with humans in sensitive contexts (healthcare, customer service, etc.), this is a critical area of research.
    *   **Methodological Approach:** The combination of quantitative sentiment analysis (VADER) with qualitative expert evaluation is a strength. It attempts to bridge the gap between automated metrics and human perception. The use of both ChatGPT and Gemini allows for a comparison of two leading LLMs.
    *   **Clear Experiment Design:** The experimental setup is well-defined. The creation of expert-authored data as a baseline is a good practice. The extension of conversations using LLMs and the creation of control groups provide a structured approach for assessing the impact of fine-tuning.
    *   **Valuable Insights:** The key finding – that structural emotional alignment isn't sufficient for genuine empathy – is valuable. It highlights the limitations of current LLMs in capturing the nuances of human emotion and understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The initial expert-authored dataset is small (24 conversations). This limits the generalizability of the findings.  While extended with LLMs, these extensions are ultimately based on a limited seed.
    *   **Expert Evaluation Subjectivity:**  The expert evaluation is, by its nature, subjective. While the use of a questionnaire provides some structure, the lack of inter-rater reliability analysis or a larger panel of evaluators weakens the robustness of this assessment. There's no discussion of the expert's background or training in empathetic listening.
    *   **VADER Limitations:**  VADER, while a useful tool, is a lexically-based sentiment analyzer. It doesn't necessarily capture the complexity and subtleties of emotional expression in dialogue. It focuses on the *what* rather than the *how* of feeling, and does not consider context and pragmatics which are fundamental to determine empathetic capacity of dialogues.
    *   **Lack of Fine-Tuning Details:**  The paper mentions "prompt engineering" but doesn't provide details about the prompts used to guide the LLMs.  A more detailed description of the prompting strategies would be helpful.  No specific fine-tuning beyond prompting is reported.
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. Other research has explored the challenges of emotional intelligence in LLMs. The use of sentiment analysis and expert evaluation is also not entirely novel.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on the limitations of LLMs in understanding and responding to human emotions.
    *   It highlights the importance of human-centered evaluation methods in this domain.
    *   The findings can inform the development of more sophisticated techniques for modeling empathy in conversational AI, potentially guiding research toward approaches that go beyond surface-level emotional mimicry.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper is well-written and addresses a relevant problem. The methodological approach is sound, but the limitations in dataset size and evaluation methodology detract from its overall impact. While the findings are valuable, they are not entirely novel and build upon existing research in the field.  The lack of detailed experimentation further limits the impact. The relatively small scope of the project also limits the scope of its influence in this domain.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 5/10

### Clarifying Before Reasoning: A Coq Prover with Structural Context
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02541v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhen Lu, Hanbin Yang, Xiaodie Wang, Ge Zhang, Biao Li, Chenxu Fu, Chao Li, Yang Yuan, Andrew Chi-Chih Yao
- **Abstract**: In this work, we investigate whether improving task clarity can enhance reasoning ability of large language models, focusing on theorem proving in Coq. We introduce a concept-level metric to evaluate task clarity and show that adding structured semantic context to the standard input used by modern LLMs, leads to a 1.85$\times$ improvement in clarity score (44.5\%~$\rightarrow$~82.3\%). Using the general-purpose model \texttt{DeepSeek-V3}, our approach leads to a 2.1$\times$ improvement in proof success (21.8\%~$\rightarrow$~45.8\%) and outperforms the previous state-of-the-art \texttt{Graph2Tac} (33.2\%). We evaluate this on 1,386 theorems randomly sampled from 15 standard Coq packages, following the same evaluation protocol as \texttt{Graph2Tac}. Furthermore, fine-tuning smaller models on our structured data can achieve even higher performance (48.6\%). Our method uses selective concept unfolding to enrich task descriptions, and employs a Planner--Executor architecture. These findings highlight the value of structured task representations in bridging the gap between understanding and reasoning.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces a novel approach to improve the reasoning ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) for theorem proving in Coq by focusing on enhancing task clarity. The authors argue that inadequate task understanding can significantly hinder reasoning performance, even when using powerful LLMs. They propose a method to add structured semantic context to the standard input used by LLMs, enriching the task description with concept unfolding and other related information. To evaluate task clarity, they introduce a concept-level metric called "clarity score," demonstrating that their structured semantic context significantly improves this score. Leveraging the general-purpose model DeepSeek-V3, they achieve a 2.1x improvement in proof success rate, outperforming the previous state-of-the-art, Graph2Tac. They also show that fine-tuning smaller models on their structured data can achieve even higher performance. The core contributions include: (1) the concept-level clarity score metric, (2) a method for enriching task descriptions with structured semantic context via selective concept unfolding, (3) a Planner-Executor architecture for leveraging the enhanced task descriptions, and (4) empirical results demonstrating significant improvements in both task clarity and theorem proving performance.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a critical, often overlooked aspect of AI reasoning: task understanding. The notion that improved clarity can enhance reasoning, independent of model scale, is a compelling and potentially transformative idea. The paper's strength lies in its well-defined methodology, comprehensive experiments, and compelling results.

*   **Novelty:** The concept of explicitly measuring and improving "clarity" as a pre-requisite to reasoning is a key contribution. The structured context enrichment, including source code, compiler internal representations, and natural language explanations, presents a novel task representation for LLMs, differing from standard text-based inputs. Selective unfolding based on a Yoneda Lemma-inspired heuristic is also a new and potentially generalizable technique.
*   **Significance:** The observed improvements in theorem proving performance are significant, especially since they use a general-purpose LLM. This suggests that the benefits are not limited to models specifically trained on mathematical data. The introduction of clarity as a tangible metric is highly valuable for evaluating future research. Moreover, the observation that the approach even allows smaller models to outperform the state of the art on the theorem-proving task is significant from a practical deployment point of view. This could enable more efficient and accessible implementations of theorem-proving systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inadequate task understanding and its impact on reasoning.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The authors provide a systematic approach for measuring clarity, enriching task descriptions, and evaluating performance.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental setup is rigorous, including ablation studies and comparisons against state-of-the-art methods. Results are carefully analyzed, supporting the paper's claims.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The demonstrated improvements in performance and efficiency have significant practical implications for automated theorem proving.
    * The paper introduces a novel metric for task clarity and a method for improving it, leading to significant improvements in theorem-proving, and providing practical insights for general-purpose LLMs and their application in similar reasoning-based domains.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Heuristic Concept Selection:** While the concept selection strategy is insightful, its reliance on clarity scores may introduce bias. A more adaptive or dynamically-adjusted selection process might prove even more effective. The Yoneda Lemma view could be further developed to ground the selection more theoretically.
    *   **Generalizability to Other Domains:** While theorem proving serves as a solid testbed, the extent to which these techniques apply to broader, less structured domains remains unclear. More discussion on adapting the techniques to different reasoning settings (e.g. robotics, medical diagnosis, code generation, etc.) would strenghten the paper.
    *   **Dependency on Existing Tools:** The approach relies heavily on the Coq compiler for extracting type-theoretic information. Adapting it to other proof assistants (e.g., Lean, Isabelle) or even non-formal domains may require significant engineering effort.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could significantly influence the direction of AI reasoning research by highlighting the importance of task understanding and providing concrete methods for improvement. It could also inspire new approaches for knowledge representation and reasoning in various domains. By emphasizing clarity over sheer model scale, the paper could shift the focus toward more interpretable and efficient AI systems.

*   **Score Justification:**  Given the paper's novel approach, thorough methodology, significant experimental results, and potentially broad impact on AI reasoning, a score of 8 is warranted. While some aspects, like heuristic concept selection and domain generalizability, require further exploration, the core ideas and contributions are highly significant. It convincingly highlights and provides solutions for the under-explored problem of task understanding.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Transformers Don't Need LayerNorm at Inference Time: Scaling LayerNorm Removal to GPT-2 XL and the Implications for Mechanistic Interpretability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02559v1)
- **Authors**: Luca Baroni, Galvin Khara, Joachim Schaeffer, Marat Subkhankulov, Stefan Heimersheim
- **Abstract**: Layer-wise normalization (LN) is an essential component of virtually all transformer-based large language models. While its effects on training stability are well documented, its role at inference time is poorly understood. Additionally, LN layers hinder mechanistic interpretability by introducing additional nonlinearities and increasing the interconnectedness of individual model components. Here, we show that all LN layers can be removed from every GPT-2 model with only a small increase in validation loss (e.g. +0.03 cross-entropy loss for GPT-2 XL). Thus, LN cannot play a substantial role in language modeling. We find that the amount of fine-tuning data needed for LN removal grows sublinearly with model parameters, suggesting scaling to larger models is feasible. We release a suite of LN-free GPT-2 models on Hugging Face. Furthermore, we test interpretability techniques on LN-free models. Direct logit attribution now gives the exact direct effect of individual components, while the accuracy of attribution patching does not significantly improve. We also confirm that GPT-2's "confidence neurons" are inactive in the LN-free models. Our work clarifies the role of LN layers in language modeling, showing that GPT-2-class models can function without LN layers. We hope that our LN-free analogs of the GPT-2 family of models will enable more precise interpretability research and improve our understanding of language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Transformers Don't Need LayerNorm at Inference Time: Scaling LayerNorm Removal to GPT-2 XL and the Implications for Mechanistic Interpretability":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the role of Layer Normalization (LN) in transformer-based language models, specifically the GPT-2 family. The authors demonstrate that LN layers can be removed from GPT-2 models, including the XL variant, with only a small increase in validation loss after fine-tuning. They then analyze the impact of removing LN on mechanistic interpretability, showing that direct logit attribution (DLA) becomes exact and that attribution patching does not significantly improve.  They also show that "confidence neurons" are inactive in LN-free models. The authors release LN-free GPT-2 models to facilitate future interpretability research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of removing LayerNorm and then fine-tuning isn't entirely novel; the authors themselves cite a precursor workshop presentation. However, the significant contribution lies in *scaling* this removal to the GPT-2 XL model and the detailed analysis of the impact on interpretability techniques. Showing that the removal generalizes to a larger model is a substantial advancement. The investigation of confidence neurons and the impact on attribution patching adds further depth.  Prior work primarily focused on smaller models or approximated LayerNorm.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work is multi-faceted:

    *   **Interpretability:** Removing LN simplifies the model architecture and removes a significant source of non-linearity and interconnectedness, making mechanistic interpretability easier in principle. The authors demonstrate this by improving DLA, achieving exact results and showing that LN layers are causing derivative issues.
    *   **Understanding LN:** The paper provides empirical evidence that LN's role at inference time in GPT-2-like models is less crucial than previously thought. While LN is essential during training for stability, the models can function effectively without it after fine-tuning. It also confirms the role of LN's non-linearity in mediating "confidence neurons".
    *   **Practical Implications:** The release of LN-free models is a valuable resource for the interpretability community, facilitating more precise analyses and potentially leading to new insights into how these models work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough empirical validation of the LN removal process, demonstrating it across multiple GPT-2 model sizes.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The impact of LN removal on various interpretability techniques is analyzed carefully, providing concrete evidence of the benefits and limitations of the approach.
    *   **Resource Contribution:**  Releasing the LN-free models is a significant contribution that lowers the barrier to entry for interpretability research on these architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The study is primarily focused on the GPT-2 family of models.  While this is a reasonable starting point, it remains to be seen whether the findings generalize to other transformer architectures or significantly larger models.  The authors acknowledge this limitation, stating, "In the future, we would like to expand our LN removal protocol to more recent models."
    *   **Fine-Tuning Requirements:** The fine-tuning process required for LN removal can be computationally expensive, particularly for larger models. While the authors show that the amount of fine-tuning data scales sub-linearly, parameter-efficient techniques will be needed to apply the methodology on more recent models. The paper could benefit from more insight on why performance remains comparable after fine-tuning. Are the linear transformations simply adapting to perform the LN scaling? Are other parts of the network compensating? How does the auxiliary loss affect this?
    *   **Overconfidence:** The paper notes that all of their LN-free models exhibit significant overconfidence, suggesting additional contributing factors that need to be further explored. This needs more explanation as it shows the LN free models are not entirely the same as the original ones.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a strong potential to influence future research in mechanistic interpretability. The simplification of the model architecture and the improved accuracy of DLA make it easier to analyze and understand the behavior of transformer-based language models. This can lead to new insights into how these models learn and represent knowledge. The LN-free models serve as valuable tools for verifying hypotheses and developing new interpretability techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental novelty (building upon a prior workshop presentation) but strong empirical validation, significant contribution in *scaling* to GPT-2 XL, thorough analysis of interpretability implications, and valuable resource contribution, I assign a score of 7. While not a complete game-changer, the paper provides a substantial step forward in making transformers more amenable to mechanistic interpretability and it provides a useful resource to the community. It has clear limitations in scope and unanswered questions, but the presented work is sound.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMREI: Automating Requirements Elicitation Interviews with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02564v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Korn, Samuel Gorsch, Andreas Vogelsang
- **Abstract**: Requirements elicitation interviews are crucial for gathering system requirements but heavily depend on skilled analysts, making them resource-intensive, susceptible to human biases, and prone to miscommunication. Recent advancements in Large Language Models present new opportunities for automating parts of this process. This study introduces LLMREI, a chat bot designed to conduct requirements elicitation interviews with minimal human intervention, aiming to reduce common interviewer errors and improve the scalability of requirements elicitation. We explored two main approaches, zero-shot prompting and least-to-most prompting, to optimize LLMREI for requirements elicitation and evaluated its performance in 33 simulated stakeholder interviews. A third approach, fine-tuning, was initially considered but abandoned due to poor performance in preliminary trials. Our study assesses the chat bot's effectiveness in three key areas: minimizing common interview errors, extracting relevant requirements, and adapting its questioning based on interview context and user responses. Our findings indicate that LLMREI makes a similar number of errors compared to human interviewers, is capable of extracting a large portion of requirements, and demonstrates a notable ability to generate highly context-dependent questions. We envision the greatest benefit of LLMREI in automating interviews with a large number of stakeholders.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "LLMREI: Automating Requirements Elicitation Interviews with LLMs".

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate requirements elicitation interviews, a crucial but resource-intensive and error-prone part of software development. The authors introduce LLMREI, a chatbot designed for conducting these interviews with minimal human intervention. They explore two prompt engineering approaches (zero-shot and least-to-most prompting) using the GPT-4o model. They also experimented with fine-tuning a GPT-3.5 model but abandoned it due to poor performance. The authors evaluate the chatbot's ability to minimize interview errors, extract requirements effectively, and adapt questioning to the interview context using 33 simulated stakeholder interviews. They find that LLMREI produces a similar number of mistakes as a human interviewer, can elicit a significant proportion of requirements, and demonstrates good context-dependent question generation. The authors propose using LLMREI in early stages of requirements elicitation for efficient gathering of initial stakeholder needs, followed by human-led interviews for more detailed requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem in Software Engineering. Requirements elicitation is a bottleneck, and automation through LLMs has the potential to significantly improve efficiency.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The study has a reasonably clear methodology, involving the design of the LLMREI chatbot, prompt engineering, and a controlled experiment with simulated stakeholder interviews. The authors provide adequate descriptions of the prompts used.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** The paper uses relevant evaluation metrics derived from existing literature on requirements elicitation interview mistakes, adapting them for an LLM context. They consider the quality of interviews, the effectiveness of elicitation, and the adaptability of the chatbot.
*   **Comparison to Baseline:** The study compares the performance of LLMREI to both human interviewers from another study and investigates the differences between LLMREI-long and LLMREI-short.
*   **Honest Discussion of Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of the study, including the use of student participants, simplified interview scenarios, concerns around the use of pre-trained LLMs, and the limited sample size.
*   **Replication Package:** The authors provide a replication package, increasing the transparency and reproducibility of their research.
*   **Contextualization and related works discussion** The authors draw related work clearly highlighting how the state of the art is not suitable for solving their approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simulated Stakeholders:** The use of student participants as simulated stakeholders is a major limitation. Their understanding of real-world business needs and their ability to articulate requirements accurately may differ significantly from actual stakeholders. This affects the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Limited Generalizability of Scenarios:** The scenarios used are somewhat generic. Applying LLMREI to more complex and domain-specific scenarios might yield different results.
*   **Small Sample Size:** The relatively small sample size of 33 interviews limits the statistical power of the findings. Larger-scale studies are needed to confirm the results.
*   **Subjective Evaluation:** Although the evaluation metrics are based on existing work, assessing the *quality* of interview responses remains somewhat subjective, especially in the adaptability analysis. More objective, automated methods could strengthen the evaluation.
*   **GPT-3.5 for Fine-tuning:** The decision to abandon fine-tuning based on GPT-3.5 might be premature. Fine-tuning using more recent models like GPT-4o or Llama 3 might produce better results.
*   **Limited Novelty in Prompt Engineering:** While the paper explores zero-shot and least-to-most prompting, the prompt design doesn't introduce particularly novel or sophisticated prompt engineering techniques. The prompts, while reasonably detailed, could have benefitted from more sophisticated prompting, such as chain-of-thought prompting or generation of reasoning traces.
*   **Limited Impact:** The research is conducted with general stakeholders. While the research is novel, there are many scenarios in which it is not suitable: High-stakes, complex and interpersonal areas. This heavily limits the impact.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on using LLMs in Software Engineering, specifically in requirements elicitation. It demonstrates the potential of LLMs to automate parts of the interview process, which can improve efficiency. While the specific prompt engineering techniques used are not particularly groundbreaking, the paper provides practical insights into the design of an LLM-based requirements elicitation chatbot. It demonstrates that LLMs can generate context-dependent questions and extract a reasonable number of requirements, while also making mistakes.

**Overall Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, I would rate this paper a **Score: 6**. While the problem addressed is important and the methodology is reasonably sound, the limitations of the study (simulated stakeholders, small sample size) and the limited novelty in prompt engineering temper the overall impact of the paper. The study provides a valuable initial exploration, but further research is needed to validate and extend these findings in more realistic settings with sophisticated prompting strategies and modern LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Reconstructing Close Human Interaction with Appearance and Proxemics Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02565v1)
- **Authors**: Buzhen Huang, Chen Li, Chongyang Xu, Dongyue Lu, Jinnan Chen, Yangang Wang, Gim Hee Lee
- **Abstract**: Due to visual ambiguities and inter-person occlusions, existing human pose estimation methods cannot recover plausible close interactions from in-the-wild videos. Even state-of-the-art large foundation models~(\eg, SAM) cannot accurately distinguish human semantics in such challenging scenarios. In this work, we find that human appearance can provide a straightforward cue to address these obstacles. Based on this observation, we propose a dual-branch optimization framework to reconstruct accurate interactive motions with plausible body contacts constrained by human appearances, social proxemics, and physical laws. Specifically, we first train a diffusion model to learn the human proxemic behavior and pose prior knowledge. The trained network and two optimizable tensors are then incorporated into a dual-branch optimization framework to reconstruct human motions and appearances. Several constraints based on 3D Gaussians, 2D keypoints, and mesh penetrations are also designed to assist the optimization. With the proxemics prior and diverse constraints, our method is capable of estimating accurate interactions from in-the-wild videos captured in complex environments. We further build a dataset with pseudo ground-truth interaction annotations, which may promote future research on pose estimation and human behavior understanding. Experimental results on several benchmarks demonstrate that our method outperforms existing approaches. The code and data are available at https://www.buzhenhuang.com/works/CloseApp.html.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of reconstructing close human interactions from monocular in-the-wild videos.  It observes that visual ambiguities and inter-person occlusions often hinder existing pose estimation methods, even when using large foundation models. The core idea is that human appearance can provide valuable cues to resolve these ambiguities. The paper proposes a dual-branch optimization framework: a motion branch (using a diffusion model trained to capture proxemic behavior) and an appearance branch (using 3D Gaussian splatting to model human appearances). Constraints based on 3D Gaussians, 2D keypoints, and mesh penetration are integrated to refine the reconstruction. They also introduce a new dataset, WildCHI, consisting of in-the-wild interaction videos with pseudo ground truth annotations. The experimental results demonstrate that the method outperforms existing approaches on several benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Dual-Branch Optimization:** The key novelty lies in the dual-branch optimization framework that combines proxemic priors learned by a diffusion model with an appearance-based branch using 3D Gaussian Splatting. This is a solid contribution since these two components had not been combined in this way before for this task.

*   **Appearance as a Cue:**  While the idea of using appearance is intuitive, the paper makes a specific contribution by showing how 3D Gaussian Splatting can be effectively integrated as a constraint in an optimization framework to resolve ambiguities.  The use of 3D Gaussians and the rendering/comparison strategy with the original image is also a novel technique.

*   **WildCHI Dataset:** The creation of a new dataset of in-the-wild interactions is a valuable contribution, as existing datasets are either captured in controlled environments or lack RGB information. The pseudo ground-truth generation process, while not perfect, allows for training and evaluating methods in more realistic settings.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Interaction Reconstruction:** The paper demonstrates significant improvements in reconstructing close human interactions compared to existing methods. It's especially beneficial in scenarios with visual ambiguities and occlusions. The ability to work with in-the-wild videos is an important step towards real-world applicability.
*   **Influence on Future Research:**  The proposed dual-branch framework and the WildCHI dataset could influence future research in this area by providing a new baseline and a more challenging dataset for evaluation. The explicit focus on human appearance is also an area that has been relatively underexplored.
*   **Limitations and Future Work:** The authors acknowledge limitations such as the inability to reconstruct high-quality textures in changing light conditions and the current restriction to two-person interactions. The paper discusses some future directions, such as incorporating light embedding and building an interaction dataset for crowds, which are all very good ideas.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Pseudo Ground Truth:** The reliance on pseudo ground truth is a limitation, as the accuracy of the annotations is limited. However, the authors have attempted to mitigate this by manually filtering out incorrect estimations. A more detailed description of the data filtration process is desired.
*   **Computational Cost:** Optimization-based methods can be computationally expensive. While the paper reports the optimization time, more details on the hardware setup would be beneficial.
*   **Dataset bias:** The dataset may have inherited dataset bias from TikTok videos, and videos from other sources with different characteristics may yield to different results.

**Overall:**

The paper addresses an important and challenging problem with a novel approach. The dual-branch optimization framework, the use of 3D Gaussian Splatting for appearance modeling, and the introduction of the WildCHI dataset are all valuable contributions. While there are some limitations, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

*   A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's strengths in addressing a challenging problem with a novel dual-branch approach and introducing a new dataset. The significant improvements in performance on interaction reconstruction and the potential influence on future research justify this score. The novel approach to the issue, dataset introduction and significant performance enhancement are all good indicators to a score that indicates this.
*   The score is not higher because the reliance on pseudo ground truth and the computational cost are notable limitations. The performance increases on the other datasets are good, but they do not address the fact that pseudo ground truth is used in training, making it difficult to compare. The overall paper is a good indicator of a reasonable technique, but additional experimentation is desired. The WildCHI dataset requires a lot of careful curation and filtering, which isn't quite apparent from this paper, and should be better documented.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Revisiting Active Learning under (Human) Label Variation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02593v1)
- **Authors**: Cornelia Gruber, Helen Alber, Bernd Bischl, Göran Kauermann, Barbara Plank, Matthias Aßenmacher
- **Abstract**: Access to high-quality labeled data remains a limiting factor in applied supervised learning. While label variation (LV), i.e., differing labels for the same instance, is common, especially in natural language processing, annotation frameworks often still rest on the assumption of a single ground truth. This overlooks human label variation (HLV), the occurrence of plausible differences in annotations, as an informative signal. Similarly, active learning (AL), a popular approach to optimizing the use of limited annotation budgets in training ML models, often relies on at least one of several simplifying assumptions, which rarely hold in practice when acknowledging HLV. In this paper, we examine foundational assumptions about truth and label nature, highlighting the need to decompose observed LV into signal (e.g., HLV) and noise (e.g., annotation error). We survey how the AL and (H)LV communities have addressed -- or neglected -- these distinctions and propose a conceptual framework for incorporating HLV throughout the AL loop, including instance selection, annotator choice, and label representation. We further discuss the integration of large language models (LLM) as annotators. Our work aims to lay a conceptual foundation for HLV-aware active learning, better reflecting the complexities of real-world annotation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Revisiting Active Learning under (Human) Label Variation" addresses the limitations of traditional active learning (AL) approaches in the presence of human label variation (HLV). It argues that conventional AL frameworks, which assume a single ground truth and perfect oracles, are inadequate for tasks where HLV is prevalent due to subjectivity or ambiguity. The authors survey the literature on both AL and HLV, identifying how these fields have (or haven't) addressed these issues. They then propose a conceptual framework for incorporating HLV into the AL loop, covering instance selection, annotator choice, and label representation, even considering the role of large language models (LLMs) as annotators. The paper aims to establish a foundation for HLV-aware active learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive synthesis of ideas from the AL and HLV communities and its attempt to formalize a framework for HLV-aware AL. While some individual components (e.g., using LLMs as annotators, considering annotator costs) have been explored previously, the holistic perspective is valuable. It brings together these ideas under a unified theoretical umbrella, identifying where existing assumptions fall short and suggesting necessary adaptations. The authors are careful to acknowledge related work and build on existing foundations.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to improve the effectiveness of AL in real-world NLP tasks where HLV is unavoidable. By explicitly acknowledging and modeling HLV, AL algorithms can be made more robust, efficient, and informative. The framework offers a roadmap for future research by highlighting key areas for development.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Review:** The literature review is thorough and well-organized, covering relevant research from multiple perspectives.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges posed by HLV for traditional AL.
    *   **Structured Framework:** The proposed framework provides a systematic way to think about incorporating HLV into the AL loop.
    *   **Discussion of LLMs:** The discussion of LLMs as annotators is timely and relevant, given the increasing role of these models in NLP.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Conceptual Nature:** The paper remains largely conceptual; there isn't any empirical validation of the proposed framework. While it clearly sets out research questions, it stops short of proving the practical efficacy of its model.
    *   **Limited Technical Depth:** While the framework outlines general strategies, it does not delve into the technical details of specific algorithms or models that could be used to implement these strategies.
    *   **Overlaps With Perspectivism (but doesn't deeply engage):** the paper gestures toward perspectivism but doesn't fully grapple with the significant philosophical hurdles and ongoing debates.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable synthesis and framework for approaching active learning in the presence of human label variation. Its thorough review and clear articulation of the challenges and necessary adaptations make it a useful contribution to the field. However, the lack of empirical validation and limited technical depth limit its impact. While conceptually sound, it relies on subsequent work to fully demonstrate its practical benefits. The paper's relative lack of technical contributions and limited philosophical engagement further justifies a more conservative score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### MPF: Aligning and Debiasing Language Models post Deployment via Multi Perspective Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02595v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Guan, PeiHsin Lin, Zekun Wu, Ze Wang, Ruibo Zhang, Emre Kazim, Adriano Koshiyama
- **Abstract**: Multiperspective Fusion (MPF) is a novel posttraining alignment framework for large language models (LLMs) developed in response to the growing need for easy bias mitigation. Built on top of the SAGED pipeline, an automated system for constructing bias benchmarks and extracting interpretable baseline distributions, MPF leverages multiperspective generations to expose and align biases in LLM outputs with nuanced, humanlike baselines. By decomposing baseline, such as sentiment distributions from HR professionals, into interpretable perspective components, MPF guides generation through sampling and balancing of responses, weighted by the probabilities obtained in the decomposition. Empirically, we demonstrate its ability to align LLM sentiment distributions with both counterfactual baselines (absolute equality) and the HR baseline (biased for Top Univeristy), resulting in small KL divergence, reduction of calibration error and generalization to unseen questions. This shows that MPF offers a scalable and interpretable method for alignment and bias mitigation, compatible with deployed LLMs and requiring no extensive prompt engineering or finetuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multi-Perspective Fusion (MPF), a post-training alignment framework for large language models (LLMs) aimed at mitigating bias. Built upon the SAGED pipeline, MPF leverages multi-perspective generations to identify and align LLM outputs with human-like baselines. It decomposes these baselines (e.g., sentiment distributions) into interpretable perspectives, guiding generation through sampling and balancing responses based on perspective weights derived from the decomposition.  Experiments demonstrate MPF's ability to align LLM sentiment distributions with counterfactual and HR baselines, reducing KL divergence and calibration error. The paper emphasizes MPF's scalability, interpretability, and compatibility with deployed LLMs without requiring extensive prompt engineering or fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques for bias mitigation. While post-training alignment and multi-perspective generation aren't individually novel, the integration within the SAGED framework and the emphasis on distributional alignment based on decomposed interpretable perspectives sets it apart. The approach avoids the need for model retraining or extensive prompt engineering which offers practical advantages for deployed models.  The connection to bias benchmarking through SAGED is also a differentiating factor.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical challenges of bias mitigation in deployed LLMs. Methods that avoid retraining and offer interpretability are highly valuable. Demonstrating a reduction in sentiment bias with minimal intervention makes MPF a potentially useful tool. The results on generalization to unseen questions (Valid 40) are encouraging. The application to resume screening (a practical area where bias is a serious concern) also strengthens the significance. However, as stated in the conclusion, the dependency on the construction of perspectives and the feature (sentiment in this case) limits the comprehensive use of the system.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** MPF offers a practical solution for bias mitigation in deployed LLMs, avoiding resource-intensive retraining.
    *   **Interpretability:**  The use of interpretable perspectives and the decomposition of baselines enhance transparency and understanding of biases.
    *   **Scalability:** The model-agnostic and zero-weight-update nature of MPF makes it relatively scalable.
    *   **Evaluation:** The use of both distributional (KL divergence) and calibration-based metrics provides a comprehensive evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Perspective Dependence:**  The effectiveness of MPF heavily relies on the quality and diversity of the defined perspectives. This raises questions about the robustness and generalizability of the method across different contexts and bias types. The paper mentions quality control.
    *   **Limited Feature Alignment:** As the paper acknowledges, MPF focuses on a single feature (sentiment in their experiment).  The lack of support for sequential alignment across multiple features is a limitation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The decomposition and optimization process, although model-agnostic, introduce non-trivial computational overhead that could impact real-time applications.
    *   **Limited theoretical justification:** there is limited theoretical support on why the specific sentiment distribution is the right solution, there is no discussion on the fairness of this.

*   **Potential Impact:**  MPF has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and interpretable method for post-deployment bias mitigation.  It could be a valuable tool for practitioners and researchers working on aligning LLMs with human values. However, its impact will depend on addressing the limitations related to perspective dependence and multi-feature alignment.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

MPF presents a valuable practical contribution to the field of bias mitigation in LLMs, especially for deployed models. The use of interpretable perspectives and the focus on distributional alignment are key strengths. However, the method's reliance on manually defined perspectives and its limited support for multi-feature alignment are significant weaknesses that impact its generalizability and robustness. In addition, there is no theoretical background for the selection of sentiment and distribution, or any consideration on fairness. The computational overhead introduced by the decomposition and optimization process is another concern. While the paper demonstrates promising results, further research is needed to address these limitations and fully realize MPF's potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### AC-Refiner: Efficient Arithmetic Circuit Optimization Using Conditional Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02598v1)
- **Authors**: Chenhao Xue, Kezhi Li, Jiaxing Zhang, Yi Ren, Zhengyuan Shi, Chen Zhang, Yibo Lin, Lining Zhang, Qiang Xu, Guangyu Sun
- **Abstract**: Arithmetic circuits, such as adders and multipliers, are fundamental components of digital systems, directly impacting the performance, power efficiency, and area footprint. However, optimizing these circuits remains challenging due to the vast design space and complex physical constraints. While recent deep learning-based approaches have shown promise, they struggle to consistently explore high-potential design variants, limiting their optimization efficiency. To address this challenge, we propose AC-Refiner, a novel arithmetic circuit optimization framework leveraging conditional diffusion models. Our key insight is to reframe arithmetic circuit synthesis as a conditional image generation task. By carefully conditioning the denoising diffusion process on target quality-of-results (QoRs), AC-Refiner consistently produces high-quality circuit designs. Furthermore, the explored designs are used to fine-tune the diffusion model, which focuses the exploration near the Pareto frontier. Experimental results demonstrate that AC-Refiner generates designs with superior Pareto optimality, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. The performance gain is further validated by integrating AC-Refiner into practical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AC-Refiner: Efficient Arithmetic Circuit Optimization Using Conditional Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper presents AC-Refiner, a novel framework for optimizing arithmetic circuits (adders and multipliers) using conditional diffusion models. The key idea is to frame arithmetic circuit synthesis as a conditional image generation task. The diffusion model is trained to generate circuit layouts, conditioned on target quality-of-results (QoRs). A neural cost predictor guides the denoising process toward promising designs. The generated high-quality designs are then used to fine-tune the diffusion model, focusing exploration near the Pareto frontier. Experimental results demonstrate superior Pareto optimality compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel application of conditional diffusion models to the problem of arithmetic circuit optimization. While diffusion models are established in image generation, their use in hardware design space exploration is relatively new.  The key novelty lies in: (1) Reformulating circuit synthesis as a conditional image generation problem where the "image" is a bitmap representation of the circuit. (2) Incorporating a neural cost predictor to guide the diffusion process toward high-quality designs, effectively blending a generative approach with a more targeted optimization strategy. (3) Using designs from the generated space to fine-tune the model.

* **Significance:**  Arithmetic circuit optimization is a crucial problem in digital design, impacting performance, power, and area. Existing methods, especially those relying on reinforcement learning or variational autoencoders, often face limitations in efficiency or design quality. AC-Refiner addresses these limitations by leveraging the strengths of diffusion models, especially their ability to capture complex structural patterns and generate diverse, valid designs. The reported improvements in Pareto optimality demonstrate the potential of this approach to yield significantly better circuit implementations. Validating the effectiveness within systolic arrays further emphasizes the potential application in real-world design.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Effective use of diffusion models:** The paper demonstrates the suitability of diffusion models for circuit generation, overcoming the limitations of VAEs in capturing fine-grained details and generating valid designs.
    * **Conditional generation:** The incorporation of a cost predictor and gradient guidance significantly enhances the optimization efficiency, enabling targeted exploration of the design space.
    * **Model fine-tuning:** Iteratively refining the diffusion model with discovered high-quality designs further improves its ability to generate optimal circuits.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The experimental results are thorough, comparing AC-Refiner to state-of-the-art baselines across different bit-widths and design scenarios. Ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components. The validation with systolic arrays shows the potential real-world design utility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Heuristic Legalization:** The design legalization, particularly for compressor trees, relies on heuristics.  While the authors claim these heuristics are efficient, a more rigorous approach (e.g., based on formal methods or ILP but optimized for speed) could further improve the quality of the generated designs. While not explicitly a *weakness*, this is certainly an area for future work.
    * **Scalability with even larger bit-widths:** Performance gains at bit-widths of 64 aren't enough. Testing at 128 or 256 would confirm general applicability.
    * **Technology Dependence:** The cost predictor is trained with a specific technology node (NanGate 45nm). While the methodology itself is technology-agnostic, the performance improvements are contingent on the accuracy of the cost predictor. Results in more recent technologies should be included.

* **Potential Impact:**  AC-Refiner has the potential to significantly impact the field of hardware design automation by providing a more efficient and effective method for arithmetic circuit optimization. The framework can be extended to other hardware design problems, such as logic synthesis and physical layout.  The use of generative models guided by cost predictors offers a promising direction for automated design space exploration.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field, addressing a crucial problem with a novel and well-executed approach. The use of conditional diffusion models for arithmetic circuit optimization is innovative, and the results demonstrate tangible improvements over existing methods. While some weaknesses exist, such as the heuristic legalization and technology-dependent cost predictor, they do not diminish the overall value of the work. The paper is well-written, clearly explains the methodology, and provides comprehensive experimental results. The potential for further research and applications is substantial. Given these factors, a score of 8 is warranted.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Lost in Latent Space: An Empirical Study of Latent Diffusion Models for Physics Emulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02608v1)
- **Authors**: François Rozet, Ruben Ohana, Michael McCabe, Gilles Louppe, François Lanusse, Shirley Ho
- **Abstract**: The steep computational cost of diffusion models at inference hinders their use as fast physics emulators.
